</EFFICIENCY>

<FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>
* When a user provides a file path, do NOT assume it's relative to the current working directory. First explore the file system to locate the file before working on it.
* If asked to edit a file, edit the file directly, rather than creating a new file with a different filename.
* For global search-and-replace operations, consider using `sed` instead of opening file editors multiple times.
* NEVER create multiple versions of the same file with different suffixes (e.g., file_test.py, file_fix.py, file_simple.py). Instead:

  - If you need to create a temporary file for testing, delete it once you've confirmed your solution works
  - If you decide a file you created is no longer useful, delete it instead of creating a new version
* Do NOT include documentation files explaining your changes in version control unless the user explicitly requests it
* When reproducing bugs or implementing fixes, use a single file rather than creating multiple files with different versions
</FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>

<CODE_QUALITY>
* Write clean, efficient code with minimal comments. Avoid redundancy in comments: Do not repeat information that can be easily inferred from the code itself.
* When implementing solutions, focus on making the minimal changes needed to solve the problem.

   - w has shape (6, 100)
   - b has shape (1, 100)

4. Adding b with shape (1, 100) should work due to broadcasting (b gets broadcasted to (10, 100))
5. However, when the Gemm optimization is applied, it fails with "Shape mismatch: x has 10 rows but z has 1 rows"

Key technical details:
- Error occurs in Gemm{no_inplace} operation
- The Gemm op signature seems to be: Gemm(z, alpha, x, y, beta) where it computes alpha*x*y + beta*z
- In this case: Gemm(b, 1.0, x, w, 1.0) computing 1.0*x*w + 1.0*b
- The error suggests that the Gemm op expects b (z) to have the same number of rows as x, but b has 1 row while x has 10 rows
- This indicates the Gemm optimization is not properly handling broadcasting of the bias term b

     1	"""Ops and optimizations for using BLAS calls
     2	
     3	BLAS = Basic Linear Algebra Subroutines
     4	Learn more about BLAS here:
     5	    http://www.netlib.org/blas/blast-forum/
     6	The standard BLAS libraries implement what is called "legacy BLAS" in that
     7	document.
     8	
     9	This documentation describes Aesara's BLAS optimization pipeline.
    10	
    11	Where there is a discrepancy between how things do work and how they *should*
    12	work, both aspects should be documented.
    13	
    14	There are four kinds of BLAS Ops in Aesara:
    15	    - Python implementations (this file)
    16	    - SciPy-based (blas_scipy)
    17	    - C-based (blas_c)
    18	
    19	Notes
    20	-----
    21	Unfortunately (because it's confusing) this file currently contains Ops
    22	that contain both Python and C versions.  I think it would be better to
    23	move the C implementations to blas_c so that this file is pure Python.
    24	-JB
    25	
    26	
    27	Ops
    28	===
    29	
    30	GEMM: Dot22, Dot22Scalar, GemmRelated, Gemm
    31	-------------------------------------------
    32	
    33	The BLAS GEMM operation implements Z <- a X Y + b Z,
    34	where Z, X and Y are matrices, and a and b are scalars.
    35	
    36	Dot22 is a GEMM where a=1, b=0, and Z is allocated every time.
    37	
    38	Dot22Scalar is a GEMM where b=0 and Z is allocated every time.
    39	
    40	Gemm is a GEMM in all its generality.
    41	
    42	In the future we can refactor the GemmRelated, Gemm, Dot22 and
    43	Dot22Scalar Ops into a single Op.  That new Op (Gemm2) is basically a
    44	normal Gemm, but with an additional configuration variable that says
    45	to ignore the input Z.  Setting that configuration variable to True
    46	would make Gemm2 equivalent to the current Dot22 and Dot22Scalar.
    47	This would make the file a lot easier to read, and save a few hundred
    48	lines of library, to say nothing of testing and documentation.
    49	
    50	
    51	GEMV: Gemv
    52	----------
    53	
    54	The BLAS GEMV operation implements Z <- a X Y + b Z,
    55	where X is a matrix, Y, and Z are vectors, and a and b are scalars.
    56	
    57	
    58	GER: Ger
    59	--------
    60	
    61	The BLAS GER operation implements Z <- a X' Y + Z,
    62	where X and Y are vectors, and matrix Z gets a rank-1 update.
    63	
    64	
    65	Other Notable BLAS-related Ops
    66	------------------------------
    67	
    68	SYRK is another useful special case of GEMM. Particularly SYRK preserves
    69	symmetry in the matrix that it updates.  See how the linear-algebra module uses
    70	symmetry hints before implementing this Op, so that this Op is compatible with
    71	that system.
    72	
    73	
    74	Optimizations
    75	=============
    76	
    77	The optimization pipeline works something like this:
    78	
    79	    1. identify dot22 from dot
    80	    2. identify gemm from dot22
    81	    3. identify dot22scalar from dot22 that are not gemm
    82	    4. specialize gemm to gemv where applicable
    83	    5. specialize gemm to ger where applicable
    84	    6. specialize dot22 -> gemv or ger where applicable
    85	
    86	:note: GEMM is the most canonical BLAS signature that we deal with so far, it
    87	    would be good to turn most things into GEMM (dot, inner, outer, dot22,
    88	    dot22scalar), and then to specialize from gemm to the various other L2 and
    89	    L3 operations.
    90	
    91	Identify Dot22
    92	--------------
    93	
    94	Numpy's dot supports arguments that are of any rank, and we should support that
    95	too (just for compatibility).  The BLAS optimizations work with Dot Ops whose
    96	inputs are each either vector or matrix.  So the first part of the optimization
    97	pipeline is to transform qualifying Dot Ops to Dot22 Ops. Dot22 Ops may be
    98	transformed further, but they will get implemented by a BLAS call.
    99	
   100	More precisely, Dot nodes whose inputs are all vectors or matrices and whose
</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Let me continue reading to find the Gemm class definition:<tool_call>execute_bash<arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>cd /workspace/aesara-devs__aesara__2.7 && grep -n "class.*Gemm" aesara/tensor/blas.py</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>518:class GemmRelated(COp):

   853	class Gemm(GemmRelated):
   854	    """In-place version of matrix-matrix multiplication (with accumulation).
   855	
   856	    When a and b are scalars and x, y, and z are matrices, then
   857	
   858	        gemm(z,a,x,y,b)
   859	
   860	    is similar to
   861	
   862	        b*z + a*dot(x,y)
   863	
   864	    The difference between the two is that the top form is destructive
   865	    on z, whereas the bottom form is not.  Gemm works in-place on the
   866	    storage associated with z, and the L{Variable} returned by Gemm
   867	    has a storage that will be aliased to the storage of the z
   868	    argument. Because of this in-place computation, an L{Apply} of
   869	    this op will destroy the L{Variable} z on which it operates.  (See
   870	    L{DestructiveOps} for an explanation of what destroying means in
   871	    the context of aesara graphs. See L{BlasLapackSupport} for more
   872	    optimized linear algebra operations.)
   873	
   874	    """
   875	
   876	    E_rank = "gemm only works for rank 2"
   877	    E_scalar = "gemm requires scalar argument"
   878	    E_z_uniq = "argument z aliased to x or y"  # TODO: justify / delete this
   879	    E_mixed = "gemm requires matching dtypes"
   880	    E_float = "gemm requires floating-point dtypes"
   881	
   882	    __props__ = ("inplace",)
   883	    params_type = ParamsType(
   884	        inplace=bool_t,

   892	
   893	    def __str__(self):

   896	        else:
   897	            inplace_str = "no_inplace"
   898	        return f"{self.__class__.__name__}{{{inplace_str}}}"

   925	        z, a, x, y, b = inputs
   926

   928	
   929	        # We want the gemm to be inplace. When this op is inplace, it
   930	        # declare to be inplace only on z. So to make it safe, we
   931	        # raise an error if z can be a view on x or y.
   932	
   933	        # I don't know if Aesara currently can support that case. As
   934	        # this case don't happen in our code, I won't spent time
   935	        # investigating this. So the assert is for safety.  I also
   936	        # think there is another mechanism that would prevent this,
   937	        # but I don't what to modify old code and have chance to break
   938	        # something.
   939	        if self.inplace:

The code checks that `Nx[0] != Nz[0]`, meaning it requires the number of rows in matrix X to match the number of rows in matrix Z. However, this doesn't account for broadcasting where Z can have fewer rows that get broadcasted.

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! Now I can see the key issue. At line 1184, the code creates a Gemm operation:
```python
rval = [gemm_no_inplace(L, alpha, Ml, Mr, beta)]
```

Here, `L` becomes the `z` parameter (the bias term in our case), `alpha` is the scaling factor for the dot product, `Ml` and `Mr` are the matrices to multiply, and `beta` is the scaling factor for `L`.

The problem is that the Gemm operation expects `L` (z) to have the same shape as the result of `Ml @ Mr`, but in our case, `L` (bias `b`) has shape (1, 100) while `Ml @ Mr` (x @ w) has shape (10, 100).

TEST CREATION: Reproducing the Issue<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/aesara-devs__aesara__2.7/reproduce_issue.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Script to reproduce the Gemm broadcasting issue described in the GitHub issue.
"""

import aesara
import aesara.tensor as at
import numpy as np

def test_gemm_broadcasting_issue():
    """Reproduce the Gemm broadcasting issue."""
    print("Testing Gemm broadcasting issue...")
    
    # Create test data as described in the issue
    b_v = np.ones((1, 100))
    w_v = np.ones((6, 100))
    x_v = np.ones((10, 6))

    x = at.matrix("x")
    b = at.matrix("b")
    w = at.matrix("w")

    out = at.dot(x, w) + b

    # It works fine if mode="FAST_COMPILE", which avoids introducing the GEMM Op
    print("\n=== Testing with FAST_COMPILE mode (should work) ===")
    f_fast = aesara.function([x, b, w], out, mode="FAST_COMPILE")
    aesara.dprint(f_fast, print_type=True)
    
    try:
        result_fast = f_fast(x_v, b_v, w_v)
        print(f"FAST_COMPILE result shape: {result_fast.shape}")
        print("FAST_COMPILE mode: SUCCESS")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"FAST_COMPILE mode: FAILED with {e}")

    # This should fail with the default mode due to Gemm optimization
    print("\n=== Testing with default mode (should fail) ===")
    f_default = aesara.function([x, b, w], out)  
    aesara.dprint(f_default, print_type=True)
    
    try:
        result_default = f_default(x_v, b_v, w_v)
        print(f"Default mode result shape: {result_default.shape}")
        print("Default mode: SUCCESS (unexpected!)")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Default mode: FAILED with {e}")
        return False
    
    return True

def test_numpy_reference():
    """Test what the result should be using numpy."""
    print("\n=== Testing expected result with NumPy ===")
    
    b_v = np.ones((1, 100))
    w_v = np.ones((6, 100))
    x_v = np.ones((10, 6))
    
    # This is what we expect: dot(x,w) + b with broadcasting
    result = np.dot(x_v, w_v) + b_v
    print(f"NumPy result shape: {result.shape}")
    print(f"NumPy result (first row): {result[0, :5]}")

Let me check what the C code does more carefully. The issue is in the shape checking logic. Let me look at the actual error from the original issue more carefully:<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Looking at the error messages:

1. From the original issue: "Shape mismatch: x has 10 rows but z has 1 rows"
2. From my simple test: "non-broadcastable output operand with shape (1,100) doesn't match the broadcast shape (10,100)"

Both errors point to the same fundamental issue: The Gemm operation expects the bias matrix `z` (which is `b` in our case) to have the same shape as the result of `x @ w`, but in broadcasting scenarios, `z` can be smaller and needs to be broadcasted.

Looking at the C code check_dims function:
```c
if (Nx[0] != Nz[0])
{
    PyErr_Format(PyExc_ValueError,
        "Shape mismatch: x has %%ld rows but z has %%ld rows",
        (long int)Nx[0], (long int)Nz[0]);
    %(fail)s;
}
```

This is overly restrictive. In NumPy broadcasting:
- If z has shape (1, 100) and the result of x @ w has shape (10, 100), 
- Then z can be broadcasted because dimension 0 is 1 (broadcastable) and dimension 1 matches (100 == 100)

The proper check should be:
- Nz[0] == Nx[0] OR Nz[0] == 1 (broadcastable)
- Nz[1] == Ny[1] OR Nz[1] == 1 (broadcastable)

But even that's not quite right because the output shape should be determined by the result of the operation, not by z's shape.

Actually, looking more carefully at the Gemm operation:
GEMM computes: Z = alpha * X * Y + beta * Z

The output shape should be the same as (X * Y), which is (Nx[0], Ny[1]).
But currently the code sets the output shape to be the same as Z's shape Nz.

This is the root of the problem! The Gemm operation should:
1. Check that Z is broadcastable to the shape of (X * Y) 
2. The output should have shape (Nx[0], Ny[1]), not Nz shape

Now I understand the complete picture:

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Clear Problem Statement and Solution

### 5.1 What the problem is:
The Gemm operation incorrectly assumes that the bias matrix `z` must have the exact same shape as the result of matrix multiplication `x @ y`. However, NumPy broadcasting rules allow `z` to be broadcastable to the result shape.

### 5.2 Where the problem is located:
Multiple places in `/workspace/aesara-devs__aesara__2.7/aesara/tensor/blas.py` in the `Gemm` class:
1. Line 962: `output = z.type()` - creates output with wrong shape
2. Line 996: `return [input_shapes[0]]` - infers wrong output shape  
3. Lines 634-639: C code shape checking is too restrictive
4. Lines 1025-1026: C code allocates output with wrong dimensions

### 5.3 How the test reproduces the issue:
Our test creates matrices where:
- `x`: (10, 6) 
- `w`: (6, 100)
- `b`: (1, 100) ← broadcastable bias

Matrix multiplication `x @ w` produces (10, 100), but `b` has shape (1, 100). According to NumPy broadcasting rules, `b` can be broadcasted because dimension 0 is 1.

However, Gemm expects `b` to have exactly shape (10, 100), causing the shape mismatch error.

### 5.4 Best practices for the fix:
1. Respect NumPy broadcasting semantics
2. Output shape should be `(x.shape[0], y.shape[1])` 
3. Bias matrix `z` should be broadcastable to this output shape
4. Minimal changes to preserve existing functionality
5. Proper validation that broadcasting is valid

### 5.5 How to fix the problem:
1. Modify `infer_shape` to return `(input_shapes[2][0], input_shapes[3][1])` instead of `input_shapes[0]`
2. Modify `make_node` to create output with correct shape
3. Modify C code shape checking to allow broadcasting
4. Modify C code output allocation to use correct dimensions

        z, a, x, y, b = inp
        (zout,) = out
        assert a.shape == ()
        assert b.shape == ()
        
        # Calculate the output shape: (x.rows, y.cols)
        out_shape = (x.shape[0], y.shape[1])
        
        if not params.inplace:
            # For out-of-place operation, we need to create output with correct shape
            # and broadcast z to that shape
            if z.shape == out_shape:
                z_out = z.copy()
            else:
                # Broadcast z to output shape
                z_out = np.broadcast_to(z, out_shape).copy()

   976	        
   977	        if not params.inplace:
   978	            # For out-of-place operation, we need to create output with correct shape
   979	            # and broadcast z to that shape
   980	            if z.shape == out_shape:
   981	                z_out = z.copy()
   982	            else:
   983	                # Broadcast z to output shape
   984	                z_out = np.broadcast_to(z, out_shape).copy()
   985	        else:
   986	            # For inplace operation, z must already have the correct shape
   987	            if z.shape != out_shape:
   988	                raise ValueError(f"Inplace Gemm shape mismatch: z has shape {z.shape} but should have shape {out_shape}")
   989	            z_out = z
   990	            
   991	        if z_out.shape == ():
   992	            z_out.itemset(z_out * a + b * np.dot(x, y))
   993	            zout[0] = z_out
   994	        else:
   995	            dot_result = np.dot(x, y)
   996	            if b == 0.0:
   997	                if a == 1.0:
   998	                    z_out[:] = dot_result
   999	                elif a == -1.0:
  1000	                    z_out[:] = -dot_result
  1001	                else:
  1002	                    z_out[:] = a * dot_result
  1003	            elif b == 1.0:
  1004	                if a == 1.0:
  1005	                    z_out += dot_result
  1006	                elif a == -1.0:
  1007	                    z_out -= dot_result
  1008	                else:
  1009	                    z_out += a * dot_result
  1010	            else:
  1011	                z_out *= b
  1012	                z_out += a * dot_result
  1013	            zout[0] = z_out
  1014	
  1015	    def infer_shape(self, fgraph, node, input_shapes):
  1016	        z_shape, a_shape, x_shape, y_shape, b_shape = input_shapes
  1017	        # Output shape should be (x.rows, y.cols) for matrix multiplication x @ y
  1018

"""
Test the Gemm broadcasting fix.
"""

import aesara
import aesara.tensor as at
import numpy as np

def test_original_issue():
    """Test the original issue from the GitHub report."""
    print("=== Testing Original Issue ===")
    
    # Create test data as described in the issue
    b_v = np.ones((1, 100), dtype='float64')
    w_v = np.ones((6, 100), dtype='float64')
    x_v = np.ones((10, 6), dtype='float64')

    x = at.matrix("x", dtype='float64')
    b = at.matrix("b", dtype='float64')
    w = at.matrix("w", dtype='float64')

    out = at.dot(x, w) + b

    # Test with FAST_COMPILE mode (should work)
    print("Testing FAST_COMPILE mode...")
    f_fast = aesara.function([x, b, w], out, mode="FAST_COMPILE")
    try:
        result_fast = f_fast(x_v, b_v, w_v)
        print(f"✓ FAST_COMPILE result shape: {result_fast.shape}")
        expected_sum = 6 * 1 + 1  # 6 from dot product (all ones) + 1 from bias
        print(f"Expected value: {expected_sum}, Got: {result_fast[0, 0]}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"✗ FAST_COMPILE failed: {e}")
        return False

    # Test with default mode (should now work with our fix)
    print("Testing default mode...")
    try:
        f_default = aesara.function([x, b, w], out)
        result_default = f_default(x_v, b_v, w_v)
        print(f"✓ Default mode result shape: {result_default.shape}")
        print(f"Expected value: {expected_sum}, Got: {result_default[0, 0]}")
        # Check that broadcasting worked - all rows should be the same since bias is (1,100)
        if np.allclose(result_default[0], result_default[1]):
            print("✓ Broadcasting worked correctly - rows are identical")
        else:
            print("✗ Broadcasting issue - rows differ unexpectedly")
        return True
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"✗ Default mode failed: {e}")
        return False

def test_edge_cases():
    """Test various edge cases for broadcasting."""
    print("\n=== Testing Edge Cases ===")
    
    test_cases = [
        # (x_shape, w_shape, b_shape, description)
        ((10, 6), (6, 100), (1, 100), "Original issue case"),
        ((10, 6), (6, 100), (10, 1), "Column broadcasting"),
        ((1, 6), (6, 100), (1, 100), "Single row input"),
        ((10, 1), (1, 100), (1, 100), "Single column intermediate"),
    ]
    
    success_count = 0
    for x_shape, w_shape, b_shape, desc in test_cases:
        print(f"\nTesting: {desc}")
        print(f"  Shapes: x={x_shape}, w={w_shape}, b={b_shape}")
        
        try:
            # Create test data
            x_val = np.random.rand(*x_shape).astype('float64')
            w_val = np.random.rand(*w_shape).astype('float64')
            b_val = np.random.rand(*b_shape).astype('float64')
            
            # Create symbols
            x = at.matrix("x", dtype='float64')
            w = at.matrix("w", dtype='float64')
            b = at.matrix("b", dtype='float64')
            
            # Create computation
            out = at.dot(x, w) + b
            
            # Compile and run
            f = aesara.function([x, w, b], out)
            result = f(x_val, w_val, b_val)
            
            # Verify shape is correct
            expected_shape = (x_shape[0], w_shape[1])
            if result.shape == expected_shape:
                print(f"  ✓ Result shape correct: {result.shape}")
                success_count += 1
            else:
                print(f"  ✗ Wrong shape: got {result.shape}, expected {expected_shape}")
                
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"  ✗ Failed: {e}")
    
    print(f"\nEdge cases passed: {success_count}/{len(test_cases)}")
    return success_count == len(test_cases)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    print("Testing Gemm broadcasting fix...")
    
    # Test original issue
    success1 = test_original_issue()
    
    # Test edge cases
    success2 = test_edge_cases()
    
    if success1 and success2:
        print("\n🎉 All tests passed! Gemm broadcasting fix is working.")
    else:
        print("\n❌ Some tests failed.")</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>File created successfully at: /workspace/aesara-devs__aesara__2.7/test_fix.py</tool_response><|assistant|></think><tool_call>execute_bash<arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>cd /workspace/aesara-devs__aesara__2.7 && python test_fix.py</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Testing Gemm broadcasting fix...

    @staticmethod
    def compile_str(
        module_name,
        src_code,
        location=None,
        include_dirs=None,
        lib_dirs=None,
        libs=None,
        preargs=None,
        py_module=True,
        hide_symbols=True,
    ):
        """
        Parameters
        ----------
        module_name : str
            This has been embedded in the src_code.
        src_code
            A complete c or c++ source listing for the module.
        location
            A pre-existing filesystem directory where the cpp file and .so will
            be written.
        include_dirs
            A list of include directory names (each gets prefixed with -I).
        lib_dirs
            A list of library search path directory names (each gets prefixed
            with -L).
        libs
            A list of libraries to link with (each gets prefixed with -l).
        preargs
            A list of extra compiler arguments.
        py_module
            If False, compile to a shared library, but do not import it as a
            Python module.
        hide_symbols
            If True (the default) all symbols will be hidden from the library
            symbol table (which means that other objects can't use them).

        Returns
        -------
        object
            Dynamically-imported python module of the compiled code (unless
            py_module is False, in that case returns None).

        """
        # TODO: Do not do the dlimport in this function

        if not config.cxx:
            raise MissingGXX("g++ not available! We can't compile c code.")

        if include_dirs is None:
            include_dirs = []
        if lib_dirs is None:
            lib_dirs = []
        if libs is None:
            libs = []
        if preargs is None:
            preargs = []

        # Remove empty string directory
        include_dirs = [d for d in include_dirs if d]
        lib_dirs = [d for d in lib_dirs if d]

        include_dirs = include_dirs + std_include_dirs()
        libs = libs + std_libs()
        lib_dirs = lib_dirs + std_lib_dirs()

        cppfilename = os.path.join(location, "mod.cpp")
        with open(cppfilename, "w") as cppfile:

            _logger.debug(f"Writing module C++ code to {cppfilename}")

            cppfile.write(src_code)
            # Avoid gcc warning "no newline at end of file".
            if not src_code.endswith("\n"):
                cppfile.write("\n")

        if platform.python_implementation() == "PyPy":
            suffix = "." + get_lib_extension()

            dist_suffix = distutils.sysconfig.get_config_var("SO")
            if dist_suffix is not None and dist_suffix != "":
                suffix = dist_suffix

            filepath = f"{module_name}{suffix}"
        else:
            filepath = f"{module_name}.{get_lib_extension()}"

        lib_filename = os.path.join(location, filepath)

        _logger.debug(f"Generating shared lib {lib_filename}")
        cmd = [config.cxx, get_gcc_shared_library_arg(), "-g"]

        if config.cmodule__remove_gxx_opt:
            cmd.extend(p for p in preargs if not p.startswith("-O"))
        else:
            cmd.extend(preargs)
        # to support path that includes spaces, we need to wrap it with double quotes on Windows
        path_wrapper = '"' if os.name == "nt" else ""
        cmd.extend([f"-I{path_wrapper}{idir}{path_wrapper}" for idir in include_dirs])
        cmd.extend([f"-L{path_wrapper}{ldir}{path_wrapper}" for ldir in lib_dirs])
        if hide_symbols and sys.platform != "win32":
            # This has been available since gcc 4.0 so we suppose it
            # is always available. We pass it here since it
            # significantly reduces the size of the symbol table for
            # the objects we want to share. This in turns leads to
            # improved loading times on most platforms (win32 is
            # different, as usual).
            cmd.append("-fvisibility=hidden")
        cmd.extend(["-o", f"{path_wrapper}{lib_filename}{path_wrapper}"])
        cmd.append(f"{path_wrapper}{cppfilename}{path_wrapper}")
        cmd.extend(GCC_compiler.linking_patch(lib_dirs, libs))
        # print >> sys.stderr, 'COMPILING W CMD', cmd
        _logger.debug(f"Running cmd: {' '.join(cmd)}")

        def print_command_line_error():
            # Print command line when a problem occurred.
            print(
                ("Problem occurred during compilation with the " "command line below:"),
                file=sys.stderr,
            )
            print(" ".join(cmd), file=sys.stderr)

        try:
            p_out = output_subprocess_Popen(cmd)
            compile_stderr = p_out[1].decode()
        except Exception:
            # An exception can occur e.g. if `g++` is not found.
            print_command_line_error()
            raise

        status = p_out[2]

        if status:
            tf = tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile(
                mode="w", prefix="aesara_compilation_error_", delete=False
            )
            # gcc put its messages to stderr, so we add ours now

Let me create a specific test for the issue I fixed:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/aesara-devs__aesara__2.7/test_broadcasting_fix.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Test for the Gemm broadcasting fix.
This test specifically addresses the issue reported where Gemm fails with simple broadcasting case.
"""

import numpy as np
import pytest

import aesara
import aesara.tensor as at
from aesara.compile.mode import get_mode
from aesara.tensor.blas import gemm_no_inplace


def test_gemm_broadcasting_original_issue():
    """Test the exact case from the GitHub issue."""
    # Create test data as described in the issue
    b_v = np.ones((1, 100), dtype='float64')
    w_v = np.ones((6, 100), dtype='float64') 
    x_v = np.ones((10, 6), dtype='float64')

    x = at.matrix("x", dtype='float64')
    b = at.matrix("b", dtype='float64')
    w = at.matrix("w", dtype='float64')

    out = at.dot(x, w) + b

    # It works fine if mode="FAST_COMPILE", which avoids introducing the GEMM Op
    f_fast = aesara.function([x, b, w], out, mode="FAST_COMPILE")
    result_fast = f_fast(x_v, b_v, w_v)
    
    # This should now work with default mode due to Gemm broadcasting fix
    f_default = aesara.function([x, b, w], out)
    result_default = f_default(x_v, b_v, w_v)
    
    # Results should be identical
    np.testing.assert_array_equal(result_fast, result_default)
    
    # Shape should be correct: (10, 100) 
    assert result_default.shape == (10, 100)
    
    # Value should be 7.0 (6 from dot product of ones + 1 from bias)
    expected_value = 6 * 1 + 1  # dot product result + bias
    assert np.allclose(result_default, expected_value)


def test_gemm_broadcasting_various_cases():
    """Test various broadcasting scenarios."""
    test_cases = [
        # (x_shape, w_shape, b_shape, description)
        ((10, 6), (6, 100), (1, 100), "Original issue: broadcast rows"),
        ((10, 6), (6, 100), (10, 1), "Broadcast columns"),
        ((1, 6), (6, 100), (1, 100), "Single row input"),
        ((10, 1), (1, 100), (1, 100), "Single column intermediate"),
        ((5, 3), (3, 7), (1, 7), "Smaller matrices"),
    ]
    
    for x_shape, w_shape, b_shape, description in test_cases:
        print(f"Testing: {description}")
        
        # Create test data
        x_val = np.random.rand(*x_shape).astype('float64')
        w_val = np.random.rand(*w_shape).astype('float64')
        b_val = np.random.rand(*b_shape).astype('float64')
        
        # Symbols
        x = at.matrix("x", dtype='float64')
        w = at.matrix("w", dtype='float64')
        b = at.matrix("b", dtype='float64')
        
        # Computation
        out = at.dot(x, w) + b
        
        # Test both modes
        f_fast = aesara.function([x, w, b], out, mode="FAST_COMPILE")
        f_default = aesara.function([x, w, b], out)
        
        result_fast = f_fast(x_val, w_val, b_val)
        result_default = f_default(x_val, w_val, b_val)
        
        # Results should be close (may have small numerical differences)
        np.testing.assert_allclose(result_fast, result_default, rtol=1e-10)
        
        # Check shape is correct
        expected_shape = (x_shape[0], w_shape[1])
        assert result_default.shape == expected_shape


def test_gemm_broadcasting_python_vs_c_linker():
    """Test that broadcasting works with both Python and C linkers."""
    # Test data
    b_v = np.ones((1, 100), dtype='float64')
    w_v = np.ones((6, 100), dtype='float64')
    x_v = np.ones((10, 6), dtype='float64')

    x = at.matrix("x", dtype='float64')
    b = at.matrix("b", dtype='float64')
    w = at.matrix("w", dtype='float64')

    out = at.dot(x, w) + b

    # Test with C linker (default)
    f_c = aesara.function([x, b, w], out)
    result_c = f_c(x_v, b_v, w_v)

    # Test with Python linker
    python_mode = get_mode('FAST_RUN')
    python_mode.linker.use_cloop = False
    f_python = aesara.function([x, b, w], out, mode=python_mode)
    result_python = f_python(x_v, b_v, w_v)
    
    # Results should be identical
    np.testing.assert_array_equal(result_c, result_python)
    
    assert result_c.shape == (10, 100)
    assert result_python.shape == (10, 100)


def test_gemm_inplace_broadcasting():
    """Test that inplace Gemm correctly rejects non-broadcastable cases."""    
    # Test data where inplace should work (exact shapes match)
    b_v = np.ones((10, 100), dtype='float64')  # Exact shape match
    w_v = np.ones((6, 100), dtype='float64')
    x_v = np.ones((10, 6), dtype='float64')

    x = at.matrix("x", dtype='float64')
    b = at.matrix("b", dtype='float64')
    w = at.matrix("w", dtype='float64')

    # This should work - exact shape match
    out = gemm_no_inplace(b, np.float64(1.0), x, w, np.float64(1.0))
    f = aesara.function([x, b, w], out)
    result = f(x_v, b_v, w_v)
    assert result.shape == (10, 100)

    # Test the case that should fail for inplace (broadcasting needed)
    b_v_small = np.ones((1, 100), dtype='float64')  # Needs broadcasting
    
    # For inplace with mismatched shapes, it should raise an error
    # Note: The inplace Gemm has stricter shape requirements
    from aesara.tensor.blas import Gemm
    gemm_inplace = Gemm(inplace=True)
    
    # This should fail during make_node for inplace operation
    x_sym = at.matrix("x", dtype='float64')
    b_sym = at.matrix("b", dtype='float64') 
    w_sym = at.matrix("w", dtype='float64')
    
    # Creating the node should work, but performing it with mismatched shapes should fail
    try:
        node = gemm_inplace.make_node(b_sym, np.float64(1.0), x_sym, w_sym, np.float64(1.0))
        # This should succeed since we only check basic compatibility in make_node
    except Exception as e:
        # If it fails here, that's fine - depends on implementation
        pass
